And most importantly, you never deviated from the order of your spiritual master: “If you get money, print books!” Your book distribution is constantly increasing. You gave yourself freely, sacrificing all the comforts of spending your old age in Vṛndāvana, to travel and preach throughout the world. You were completely generous with your time, always acting for the welfare of everyone. You patiently taught your disciples everything so that we could begin the nine processes of devotional service.

Now more and more devotees are coming forward to glorify you. Many who have been separated from the devotional society are returning with fond and valuable memories. These are being recorded and distributed, much to the delight of everyone. Others are new devotees but are equally inspired to spread your name and fame. I can only remember a few things, but please let me never forget that pleasing you is the only worthwhile activity, life after life. You said, “Distribute books, distribute books, distribute books.” Please allow me to enter into that mood. Let me have no other desire but to read your books and distribute your books, and to teach others to do that.

It is said that no one can understand the activities of great devotees. That is because they are acting completely under the influence of Kṛṣṇa, who is inconceivable. But your activities are always relishable, whether one can understand them or not. Please let us never forget you and always be open to your guidance. In a worldwide preaching society there are naturally so many difficulties, but you showed us how to handle everything. And you set up the mechanism to expand unlimitedly and still maintain the purity. Please let us never deviate from your lotus feet. Please forgive my unpoetic and clumsy attempts to glorify you. I am a very insignificant player in the business of glorifying you. So many devotees are publishing and distributing so many of your books. So many new temples are opening or are under construction. I pray to be allowed to always be in the association of your devotees and to desire to serve them.

Your insignificant servant,  
Kavicandra Swami

## Madhusevita dasa

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāmin iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my most humble obeisances. All glories to your divine appearance day.

Since your departure from this planet, so many events have followed one upon the other, almost without a break. Our shortcomings cannot withstand the siege of your impeccable instructions. We are forced to grow in awareness. “Taking shelter of the Spiritual Master” loses more and more the passive connotation that our ingenuity and ignorance would want to apply to its meaning. Either we actively pursue a life of total identification with your instructions, or we shall be controlled by something else. This, at least, has been proven by the brief history of this movement.

You are the irremovable and umwearying life-force of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, and the problems or setbacks we face are nothing but the consequence of our not understanding and accepting this fact.

Kali has so much determintaion and patience. He knows the tremendous potency of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement; therefore he has devised a manifold plan to make your position relative either in our personal lives or in the principles that govern the movement.

We hear talks of absurd dichotomies like institution and spiritual life, or preaching and introspection; we hear of the need to supplement your instructions to make them complete; in one case we even heard of the necessity of Christianity and its practices as the natural supporting element for advancing in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, you know my many faults and misgivings, but on this auspicious occasion I pray to you for one benediction:

Let the firm conviction that you are the plenipotentiary of the Supreme Personality of Godhead Śrī Kṛṣṇa Caitanya, empowered to push back the influence of Kali, be established in my heart as an irrevocable fact, and let me be actively engaged in your service.

Madhusevita dāsa will double his efforts to become an honest and surrendered soul at your lotus feet.

Your last and insignificant servant,  
Madhusevita dāsa

## Mukunda Goswami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

**“I offer my respectful obeisances unto His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, who is very dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa, having taken shelter at His lotus feet.”**

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

**“Our respectful obeisances are unto you, O spiritual master, servant of Sarasvatī Gosvāmī. You are kindly preaching the message of Lord Caitanyadeva and delivering the Western countries, which are filled with impersonalism and voidism.”**

I am beginning to understand what you meant when you said you practically had to “walk through fire” to start this movement.

I am beginning to understand how difficult it must have been to begin a spiritual movement in the most materialistic corner of the material world. I am beginning to understand from you the meaning of tolerance. Even when things turned very wrong for ISKCON, you were always merciful and friendly.

I am beginning to understand the nature of great saints and mystics who accomplish impossible tasks by the strength of their oneness in purpose with the desires of the Supreme Lord.

I am beginning to understand how it was possible for Lord Brahmā to create the universe through the mercy and intelligence bestowed upon him by the Supreme Lord.

I am beginning to understand that you could tolerate almost every imaginable problem and failure—lawsuits, cheaters, broken promises, uncultured followers, mistakes—while never becoming perturbed, always remaining cheerful and gentle.

I am beginning to understand that when you were meeting with us in your room in London, you never stopped thinking of your movement’s progress all over the world. You had probably answered ten letters that day—letters that expertly guided your disciples in many countries on difficult and crucial issues; you answered even the most tangled questions perfectly. And these answers often dramatically affected the future course of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

I am beginning to understand that when we sat so comfortably with you in your Los Angeles room, confident that we had your undivided attention, you were also meditating on the progress and problems in Bombay, London, Tokyo, Sydney, Rio, Rome, Paris, and many other cities.

I am beginning to understand that as you were writing the “law books for mankind” for the next ten thousand years, you were simultaneously managing your worldwide organization: counseling disciples, preaching to academics and other important persons, and giving us deeper and deeper spiritual realizations through your preaching. You never stopped handing us new and exciting initiatives and visions of the future.

I am beginning to understand how you embodied all the qualities of a *mahā-bhāgavata,* an *ācārya* who sets the tone of a universal preaching mission that continues for thousands of years.

I am beginning to understand how your abilities were beyond ordinary human ones. In the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* (*Madhya-līlā* 16.65, purport) you state that “Even more difficult tasks \[than those performed by Hanumān and Arjuna\] can be performed by the preachers remaining faithful to the service of the Lord.” You have indeed performed such tasks. This has been your miracle, Śrīla Prabhupāda. But you have gone a step further. You have performed some tasks that establish goals which stretch hundreds of years into the future, by giving to us, your followers, and the followers yet to be born, projects and programs to develop and realize for many centuries.

I am beginning to understand how you were a friend to everyone. When we first met I honestly thought you were my only real friend, the only person who really cared about me—spiritually, materially, mentally, emotionally, psychologically. But you seemed to be just as friendly to others; they testified to this. You were *dhīrādhīra-jana-priyau priya-karau,* a true friend to both refined and uncouth human beings. Sometimes I encounter people who, having met you only once many years ago, can never forget you.

I am beginning to understand how your friendship extended far beyond the circle of people you met and spoke with or lectured to. Through the mass distribution of your books, you gave and still give to the world the gift of friendship between human beings as well as the greatest friendship—between each human being and the Supreme Entity, Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa.

I am beginning to understand that even though your fledgling movement was often beset with many problems, you never withdrew your kindness. You were always ready to sacrifice any personal inconvenience for the happiness and well-being of others.

I am beginning to understand what it must mean to remember Kṛṣṇa at every moment, to never forget Him, and to be continuously immersed in transcendental love for the Lord. Your every movement, expression, and gesture was graceful and poetic; such simple things as a gesture of your hand, your walk, the way you signed your name, the way you intoned certain words or phrases, the way you sat down—even such simple things conveyed volumes of understanding and meaning about the spiritual world, where every walk is a dance, and every word a song.

I am beginning to understand that you brought forth part of the spiritual world with your every word and deed. You made the spiritual world a reality for us who had no knowledge of anything beyond this world of duality.

I am beginning to understand how, from the very start, you carefully orchestrated your plan, long before you had the players to administrate and execute the strategies. Then Kṛṣṇa Himself sent you manpower, brain power, and money to execute the Lord’s work of widening His circle of followers.

I am beginning to understand how you never held ill feelings toward anyone, even if someone insulted you. Once a lady, listening to you on a call-in radio program, phoned up to call you a “freeloader,” a beggar, and you jovially replied, “Yes,” happily admitting that you were. She was unable to respond, having been disarmed by your serenely confident answer.

I am beginning to understand how you never compromised, and how you never acted independently of the desires of Lord Kṛṣṇa and your Guru Mahārāja.

I am beginning to understand how you were (and are) the living *bhāgavata,* the person *bhāgavata.* Your whole being and every word you uttered was based on the eternal teachings of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, on the words spoken by Lord Kṛṣṇa Himself and by His pure servants.

I am beginning to understand how you represent much more than just yourself—a *mahātmā,* or great soul. You are the embodiment of the complete range of Vedic knowledge (*mahājano yena gataḥ sa panthā*) from time immemorial.

I am beginning to understand how your merciful benediction on this fallen and useless soul is the only merit I possess.

Your eternal servant,  
Mukunda Goswami

## Nirañjana Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my prostrated obeisances in the dust of your lotus feet. All glories to Your Divine Grace.

This morning while reading and then speaking from one of your *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* purports, by your mercy I was again able to catch a glimpse of the limitless realization each of your purports contains. Although my own realizations are always inadequate, never reaching the depths of your most confidential relationship with Kṛṣṇa, still I can say that your *prakaṭa-līlā*, displaying such full devotion and surrender to Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura and Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, are so satisfying to hear, chant, and remember that they sustain my life in this material world. These pastimes are so deep and unlimited.

Lord Kṛṣṇa says that there is no servant in this world who is more dear to Him than one who explains His confidential teachings to His devotees. Therefore not only are you the most dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa, but you are the most confidential servant of the Lord, too. Therefore, when I’m hearing, chanting, or remembering these pastimes or your instructions, I find shelter in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

In the Ninth Canto, Chapter 4, text 64, you have written, “The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is an attempt to increase the number of pure devotees of the Supreme Lord more and more. It is certain that a devotee who helps in this endeavor to satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead becomes indirectly a controller of the Supreme Lord.” If anyone helps *you,* Śrīla Prabhupāda, the pure devotee whose association purifies others, then certainly these words are realized, because you directly control Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa is controlled by the love of His pure devotees, and thus you always have Kṛṣṇa waiting to serve you, bringing everything you need to execute this most confidential service for Him.

So this morning I read and spoke from the following purport from the 6th Canto:

“An intelligent man will never aspire to become a devotee to achieve material happiness. That is the test of a devotee. As Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu teaches:

*na dhanaṁ na janaṁ na sundarīṁ*  
*kavitāṁ vā jagad-īśa kāmaye*  
*mama janmani jamanīśvare*  
*bhavatād bhaktir ahaitukī tvayi*

**‘O almighty Lord, I have no desire to accumulate wealth, now do I desire beautiful women, nor do I want any number of followers. I only want Your causeless devotional service, birth after birth.’**

A pure devotee never begs the Lord for material happiness in the shape of riches, followers, a good wife, or even *mukti*. The Lord promises, however, *yoga-kṣemaṁ vahāmy aham:* “I voluntarily bring everything necessary for My service.” (end of purport)

I became so inspired by the way you translated *yoga-kṣemaṁ vahāmy aham*. Not because it was comforting to know that Kṛṣṇa will voluntarily bring everything necessary to me for His service when I become *nityābhiyuktānām,* or “always fixed in devotion.” But I was inspired by the realization that your disciples were brought to your lotus feet because you only wanted the Lord’s causeless devotional service, birth after birth. This was so perfectly displayed in the preaching pastimes you manifested before us.

In a letter you have written, “You are all my children, and I love my American boys and girls who are sent to me by my spiritual master, and I have accepted them as my disciples.” You did not want followers, but because you were so fixed on serving the order of your Guru Mahārāja, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī, and Lord Kṛṣṇa Himself, the Lord “voluntarily” brought you followers for serving Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta’s order: *yoga-kṣemaṁ vahāmy aham*. Kṛṣṇa did not have to arrange this. He voluntarily made this arrangement for His confidential devotee.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, I know only too well that although for some inconceivable reason you have accepted me as your disciple, Kṛṣṇa did not pick me to come and assist you. His selection of servants for you were those who assisted you, and those who are assisting you now. As for me, I cannot be counted among them, for I realize that I have yet to assist you.

Therefore on this most auspicious day of your divine appearance in this material world, I bow down to your lotus feet and beg you to please give me the opportunity to go on with my insignificant attempt to be counted among those who are really your servants. Please let me serve those who are your servants. And if I must stay here in this material world, attempting to serve your order birth after birth, please bless me so that I may go on hearing, chanting, and remembering your limitless pastimes of reclaiming the fallen souls in this material world. Whether someday Kṛṣṇa will take notice of my insignificant efforts to serve you or not, is between you and Kṛṣṇa. If that day ever comes, and I can actually render some valuable service, then let me now openly declare that it was you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, who rescued this insignificant soul from the mire of material life. I want nothing else but to be a part of your eternal pastimes, and thus I go on praying and hoping that someday I will actually become,

Your eternal servant,  
Nirañjana Swami

## Param-gati Swami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāmin iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

It’s actually impossible to glorify you appropriately, for your qualities are like the waves of the ocean. Yet by the causeless mercy of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, we are able to see just a glimpse of your true position. You are so powerful that simply by thinking about your qualities and behavior, the mind becomes purified.

Gradually the world will understand who you really are, though this will be possible only if your instructions are followed. How is it possible for someone to know you? By reading very carefully your instructions, and knowing you, we will be able to see the Personality of God, Śrī Kṛṣṇa and His associates. You wrote these books especially for your disciples and followers. And the more we are serious in following your instructions, the more the whole human society will gradually adopt the Hare Kṛṣṇa chant and worship Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

*naiṣāṁ matis tāvad urukramāṅghriṁ*  
*spṛśaty anarthāpagamo yad-arthaḥ*  
*mahīyasāṁ pāda-rajo-’bhiṣekaṁ*  
*niṣkiñcanānāṁ na vṛṇīta yāvat*

**“Unless they smear upon their bodies the dust of the lotus feet of a Vaiṣṇava completely free from material contaminations, persons very much inclined toward materialistic life cannot be attached to the lotus feet of the Lord, who is glorified for his uncommon activities. Only by becoming Kṛṣṇa conscious and taking shelter at the lotus feet of the Lord in this way can one be freed from material contamination” (*****Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*** **7.5.32).**

Your eternal and unworthy servant,  
Param-gati Swami

## Prabhaviṣṇu Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my most humble obeisances on this most auspicious day of your appearance. All glories to Your Divine Grace.

Here in this material realm, all living beings are caught in a labyrinthine web of fruitive activity, trying to squeeze some insignificant pleasure from the senses. They struggle in this manner for a few short years, only to be swallowed mercilessly by cruel death and again regurgitated in another material body, to continue their meaningless pursuit of sense gratification. To break free from this endless cycle of suffering is practically impossible, unless one is rescued by the divine intervention of the Lord.

How does the Lord extract the conditioned soul from this predicament? He does it by sending his pure representative to teach the scientific process of devotional service, by which one can give up this attraction for the mirage of material existence and develop attachment to the lotus feet of the Lord.

You are that supremely pure devotee, Śrīla Prabhupāda; you have given real life and love to us, who were parched in the empty desert of the mundane world. You have revived us by giving us the holy name of Kṛṣṇa, the elixir of immortality. You have given us the sweet nectar of the transcendental pastimes of Lord Caitanya and Lord Kṛṣṇa in the form of your books. You have given us a genuine spiritual society in the form of ISKCON, where one can always relish the uplifting association of the devotees and thus be protected from the ravaging association of the exploitative materialists. You have given us the incredible fortune of being able to render personal service to the lotus feet of the Lord, present as the resplendently beautiful Deities in your temples. And you have even given us the chance to associate with Kṛṣṇa through that activity which we are all so attached to—eating—for you have taught us how to respect *prasādam*. In other words, you are our savior, who is rescuing us from the devouring jaws of Kali-yuga. Therefore you are our real life and soul, and to constantly meditate on you through service is the perfection of our existence.

So on this auspicious day of your Vyāsa-pūjā ceremony we humbly pray for the determination to remain always attached to your lotus feet, always absorbed in your service with full enthusiasm. Let us use every drop of our energy—mental, vocal, and physical—to assist you in your sublime mission of spreading Lord Caitanya’s message to every nook and corner of the world. Thus, just as you have given us the chance to attain freedom from Māyā, let us gladly offer that opportunity to everyone else, in the shape your books, the holy name, *prasādam,* and the association of the Deities and the devotees. We have embarked, by your grace, on the voyage back to Godhead, so let us not falter or turn back, but let us ever deepen our absorption in Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa.

Your mercy on us is unlimited, Śrīla Prabhupāda. Let us never forget that we are eternally indebted to you, and let us always show our gratitude by serving you in full and unconditional surrender. Let us live forever in the soothing shade of your divine lotus feet.

Your insignificant servant,  
Prabhaviṣṇu Swami

## Rādhānātha Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my respectful obeisances at your most worshipful lotus feet.

I pray that by your causeless mercy I may have the fortune of eternally residing as a particle of dust at the lotus feet of your servants. Those precious souls who have come to serve your divine mission are certainly very dear to you. I pray that I may perceive all such fortunate souls as your worshipable representatives.

In our Vaiṣṇava society Lord Kṛṣṇa arranges many forms of relationships—your honored Godbrothers and friends; all of my Godbrothers and Godsisters, from exalted seniors, intimate friends, to those struggling on the path; disciples, well-wishers, and those just approaching the path of *bhakti*. I pray that I may be blessed with the single-pointed consciousness of being their humble servant in whichever role they allow me to serve them. The supreme treasure of creation is to assist your servants with my life and soul, for the pleasure of Your Divine Grace.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, you have personified the supreme compassion of Śrī Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Your selfless sacrifice to deliver His mercy is most intimately dear to the lord. We must have great faith that by simply assisting and pleasing you, Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Śyāmsundara’s most intimate blessings will be upon us. You have so graciously made it simple and clear how we could achieve the supreme perfection of life: to strictly follow the devotional principles of hearing and chanting according to your direction; to strive to practice and realize the crest jewel of all teachings—*tṛṇād api su-nīcena*—to wholeheartedly give our lives, according to our position, to assist you in preaching Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s *saṅkīrtana* movement; to show our love for you by how we cooperate with one another.

You have given us entrance into the line of supreme grace, the Gauḍīya sampradaya from Śrī Rupa and Raghunatha is an incessant flow of Kṛṣṇa Prema descending through the mercy of our most worshipable acaryas. Your Divine Grace has given us connection to that divine realm. We are gratefully indebted to you for all eternity.

I sincerely fall at your lotus feet and beg for your kindness. I have caused much pain to my God Brothers and God Sisters. In the line of your compassion they have once again accepted me as their servant. I beg forgiveness from them and extend my deepest gratitude for their kindness. I pray that I may have no aspiration except to assist them in being an instrument in your loving hands.

On this most auspicious day I offer my life and the lives of all those accepting direction from me at the most worshipable feet of Your Divine Grace.

Your servant  
Rādhānātha Swami

## Ravīndra Svarūpa dāsa

Dearest Śrīla Prabhupāda,

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāmin iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Please accept my fallen *daṇḍavats* at your lotus feet again and again. I have no other shelter than your feet. Nor shall there be any other shelter for the entire world save those same feet that shelter and give life to me.

I make this sweeping claim soberly, although critics may accuse me of exaggeration or hyperbole. This is how I would explain your position to them:

Lord Caitanya is the *yuga-avatāra,* who descended to execute His infallible mission. His goal was to establish everywhere in the world *hari-nāma-saṅkīrtana* as the *yuga-dharma* for the Age of Kali. The *Brahma-vaivarta Purāṇa* records Kṛṣṇa’s prediction to Gaṅgā-devī that His devotees will flourish for ten thousand years in Kali-yuga. How is it possible for devotees to flourish in this evil age? By the mercy of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

Thus, after Lord Caitanya’s advent, even as Kali increased, the “Golden Age” of Kṛṣṇa consciousness paced him, increasing simultaneously, step by step. In that process, Kali’s major triumph—the global hegemony of the modern West—has been co-opted by Kṛṣṇa for His own purposes. (In this way, Kṛṣṇa made the external material potency work as internal spiritual potency).

The triumph of the modernized West had bound the world more and more together by means of continually improving technologies of transport and communication. When traditional cultures and civilizations became gathered within the prodigious octopus-grasp of Modernity, they began to intermingle and interpenetrate, even as Modernity engaged in dissolving and digesting their ancient substances. This event set the stage and prepared the means for the “breakout” of Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s movement, for its setting forth into the world from its maternal home, India.

To retrofit Lord Caitanya’s movement for its setting forth became the task of three empowered spiritual architects and renovators of three generations: Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Prabhupāda, and Śrīla Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda. When finally the story of the progressive achievement of these three geniuses is fully told, it will form an epic narration, a saga of spiritual adventure to place on the shelf beside the *Rāmāyaṇa* and *Mahābhārata*. It will fascinate the world for centuries to come. I can only give the barest hint here.

Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura:

He rescued Lord Caitanya’s movement. Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura had the power to discriminate the genuine flowering creeper of Lord Caitanya’s movement, which was nearly lost among the rank growths of well-established weeds of various *apasampradāyas*. These mimics displayed perversities that had brought Lord Caitanya’s very name into international disrepute. Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura uncovered, rejuvenated, propagated, and defended the authentic teachings and practices of Lord Caitanya.

Moreover, Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura profoundly grasped both the challenge and the opportunity presented by the world-dominance of the modern West. He studied Western thinkers and confronted their ideas with the teachings of Lord Caitanya. He envisioned the movement of Lord Caitanya acting on a global scale. In this way Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura effected the intellectual transfer of Lord Caitanya’s movement into a radically new cultural context. He did this because, above all, he was possessed by an unrelenting drive to deliver pure Kṛṣṇa consciousness to all people, by all means, and at any cost.

Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Prabhupāda:

He inherited from his father, Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura, the same drive to preach. He was animated by his father’s realization of how Kṛṣṇa consciousness could—and would—spread throughout the world. Therefore, Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Prabhupāda gave concrete form to the vision of Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura by creating the Gauḍīya Maṭha as a practical instrument for active preaching in the world.

In so doing, he became a religious revolutionary. His father had uncovered the authentic Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in the shape of a religion of reclusive, *mahā-bhāgavata bābājīs*. Now his son transferred the elixir of this authentic Kṛṣṇa consciousness into a new container. He transposed a religion on the platform of *mahā-bhāgavatas* to one on the platform of *madhyama-adhikārīs* and thus produced an active preaching mission directed toward all people, whatever their class, caste, or cultural level. This entailed practical adjustments in *sādhana* and ritual. It was a transformation, but it was, as they say in philosophy*, salve veritate:* it preserved truth. The established groups of compromised, parochial, or merely complaisant Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas naturally could not see this. They criticized Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Prabhupāda. He took them on. He challenged all the *apasampradāyas* and defeated them.

He embraced the most practical means of spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Book distribution was the foremost of his endeavors to engage modern technologies, especially of transport and communications, for preaching. Modern, secular things should not be rejected if they can be used in furthering Lord Caitanya’s mission. To drive the point home, he drove up to Rādhā-kuṇḍa, the holiest of holies, in a Jaguar sedan, scandalizing the scandalous *bābājīs* there.

He also had the insight to realize that for a unified preaching institution to operate effectively in the modern world, on a global scale, and in a stable and enduring manner, it needed to be managed in the modern way, by a board of directors, rather than in the more traditional manner of a sole autocratic *ācārya*. Unfortunately, the failure of his leading disciples in the Gauḍīya Maṭha to understand this point led to the disintegration of the Mission after Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī’s disappearance.

Śrīla Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, our spiritual master and founder-*ācārya*:

All of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s achievements demonstrably rest on one prime quality: He fully embraced his spiritual master’s order and made it his “life and soul.” As a consequence, Śrīla Prabhupāda was given the confidence, courage, and stamina to take that great leap of faith. All alone he crossed the dark waters and brought Lord Caitanya’s movement to America. In reciprocation, Kṛṣṇa sent thousands of lost souls to him to save. It was hard work; it was suffering: “I have to shed my blood three tons to make one convinced in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. That is my experience … especially these Europeans and Americans.” Śrīla Prabhupāda shed his blood endlessly.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was empowered with the vision, the knowledge, and the compassion to fulfill the innermost desires of Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura and Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura. He worked only for twelve years, with crude and unmalleable material (us, his disciples), and yet the whole world heard the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra,* and the *Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* found their way into every nook and corner of the globe. Carrying Lord Caitanya’s mission as far as he was able, Śrīla Prabhupāda broke new ground practically every day; consequently, he was challenged repeatedly to make the correct decision. At stake was the vital issue: how to preserve and perpetuate unchanged the teachings of Caitanya Mahāprabhu in unprecedented circumstances. By virtue of his faithful following, Śrīla Prabhupāda was given the intelligence to meet these challenges.

He worked hard to set up his preaching institution, the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, formed carefully after the directions of his Guru Mahārāja. He set up and trained a Governing Body to continue ISKCON after him. He called ISKCON his body, and it indeed continues after him, still animated by his indwelling, ever-watchful spirit. He will remain thus with us as long as we ourselves make his order our own life and soul.

This is my case, Śrīla Prabhupāda. Your acts have proven that you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, above all, have inherited from Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Prabhupāda the urgent desire to preach despite all risks. You have proven it.

Now, I understand that your Guru Mahārāja was liberal with his order to preach in the West. He gave it to many. He badly wanted it accomplished. I do not know why, but you were the only one who showed the fixed determination to carry through with it, no matter what. You could have cultivated your *bhajana* in bliss and ease, and gone back to Godhead without trouble. Yet, you gained a further purpose from the order of your *guru,* which you took up with ferocious determination.

If not for you, and you alone, Śrīla Prabhupāda, I would now be dead—or better off dead. I know where I was headed. If not for you, no one would have come for me and saved me. No one would have answered my cries. The angelic orders would have remained indifferent.

You have received the legacy from Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Prabhupāda, and I know that Lord Caitanya’s movement will only advance through those who receive the same legacy, in the same way, from you. Therefore I say that when Lord Caitanya’s movement gives shelter to the whole world, it will be because all have taken shelter of those who have taken shelter at your feet.

The Catholic Church claims *extra ecclesia non salus est*, “There is no salvation outside the Church.” I wouldn’t presume to make a similar claim. Outside of your followers, the Prabhupādānugas, there may well be elevated souls who go back to Godhead. That is not the issue. Rather, who will be enabled to spread the *saṅkīrtana* movement? I say that unless a person takes full shelter of you or of those who have taken shelter of you, no one can be empowered with the potency to push on Lord Caitanya’s mission.

Those who receive that potency from you will not care what becomes of the future of their lives. They will not mind shedding their blood by the ton to save one soul. They will live their lives solely for the sake of others and be unconcerned about their own material or spiritual destination.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, please give me the unflagging desire to preach without ceasing, the magnanimity to live only for the sake of others, the courage to carry your movement into new and uncharted territories, the intelligence to find the ways and means to do it, and the willingness to risk everything to accomplish your order. In this way, Śrīla Prabhupāda, bless me to become your servant.

Hoping to receive your mercy, I am  
Your aspiring servant,  
Ravīndra Svarūpa dāsa

## Rohiṇīsuta dāsa

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

To love the spiritual master and always try to execute his instructions are the supreme duties of a bona fide disciple, which will lead him to the supreme perfection: love of Godhead. It sometimes sounds easy, but actually it is most difficult.

This is because love is not under our control and has nothing to do with our mind, senses, and intellect—love is unmotivated, causeless, and flows like a nectar stream uninterrupted from the heart and floods everything. When there is real love, one is not the same anymore. This is actually the second birth; this is real life. At that time one has only one interest: to satisfy the spiritual master and to always hear, chant, and remember his glories. The disciple gives everything for the *guru’s* pleasure. His body, mind, and words are fully engaged. He is like the *guru’s* dog, always ready to do everything the spiritual master commands, and always ready to protect his dear master and his possessions, even by risking his life, as in the glorious example of Kureśa, who disguised himself as his spiritual master to meet a king who wanted to force his spiritual master to give up Vaiṣṇavism and become a Śaivite.

Because Kureśa didn’t agree and defeated the king with arguments from *śāstra,* the king’s henchmen plucked out his eyes. Later on his *guru* instructed him to pray to the Lord in the temple to get back his eyesight. But Kureśa didn’t think about himself. The Lord asked him, “What can I give you?” He replied, “Please forgive my tormentor, the king.” “So be it,” said the Lord.

Later on, when Kureśa met his Guru Mahārāja, his *guru* told him, “My dear Kureśa, I am very glad to hear about the ecstasy you are feeling from delivering the person who wronged you. However, in this you have shown great selfishness by keeping the happiness to yourself. Now you must make me happy as well by begging the Lord for the return of your sight. Do you not know that your body and mind are my property? Thus it is your duty to maintain them on my behalf.”

Kureśa then felt very jubilant at heart, thinking, “Now my Guru Mahārāja has accepted me as a fully surrendered servant.” He immediately hastened to the temple. There the Lord asked him again, “What is your desire?” Kureśa answered, “Some time ago I lost two valuable possessions belonging to my master. By your grace I would like to recover them.”

The Lord replied, “Certainly two new eyes will now adorn your body, and may they bring unlimited pleasure to your master. Just as My devotees desire only to serve Me, so I take great pleasure in supplying everything to My devotees. In this way our exchanges of love become sweeter and sweeter.”

There are so many examples of great Vaiṣṇavas serving their spiritual masters single-mindedly. But the greatest example of love for one’s *guru* and determination to serve him with body, mind, and words is you, Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Where can we start or stop in speaking about your glories? They are unlimited. Your words, deeds, and personality are simply wonderful. It is just wonderful to think about you, read about you, speak about you, read your books—which are nondifferent from you—and to distribute you in the form of your books. As long as we can do that, there are no problems. Please, therefore, let me be more and more absorbed in your mood of serving the spiritual master and pleasing him.

Although I am very weak, insignificant, and foolish, please engage me always in your service cooperatively with all the Vaiṣṇavas who serve you with full love and devotion. To associate with them means to have your association. You are always in their hearts. Not only your direct disciples but all your followers in your great family have so much love for you. They distribute unlimited numbers of books, and they have no other interest than pleasing you. It is wonderful to see how you send and empower more and more favorable souls to preach Lord Caitanya’s mission.

Also, the congregation and all those who read your books—they all love you so much and love what you have given them.\* They may find fault in us, but they cannot find fault in you. You are Kṛṣṇa’s man, who appeared like the sun in the darkened twentieth century of Kali-yuga to destroy all the darkness. You are like the Ganges. It is said that even the most impure *caṇḍāla* can become pure, provided he takes a bath in the Ganges. Similarly, provided we meditate on you, follow your instructions, and cooperate to push forward your mission, all of us will be purified and feel ecstatic. Please, Śrīla Prabhupāda, never push me away from you; otherwise I may forget you and your mission. I am still on the bank of your Ganges-like lotus feet, awaiting your merciful push so that I will fall into the transcendental nectar of pure loving devotional service.

*Gaura-premānande hari-haribol!*

All glories to Śrīla Prabhupāda!

Your fallen servant,  
Rohiṇīsuta dāsa

\*P.S. Here are three letters from readers of your books in Russia:

Dear Brothers,

Please let me express profound respect for you. Accept my humble, love-filled obeisances. All of us are united in one eternal duty, one goal in life—the service of Lord Kṛṣṇa.

My name is Oleg. After graduating from secondary school, I would like to join one of the centers for Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Please tell me what the living conditions are. Should I first learn a mundane profession? Should I first go through the military service in the army?

The desire to renounce everything material and devote my life to spiritual perfection was awakened when I read the books of A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda. By his divine grace, the fire of devotional service was kindled in me. I express my profound respect unto him.

Dear brothers—now with all my doubts burned away, I’m aspiring to enter your society of pure devotees of Lord Kṛṣṇa. I ask you to give me complete information about your society so that I can come to you and continue my service to the Lord in the association of pure devotees.

With great humility I am waiting for your answer. Hare Kṛṣṇa.

Oleg Belkin  
Severo-onezhsk

Dear Sirs,

The books published by the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust completely changed my world view. Lord Kṛṣṇa is really present in these books. The instructions of His Divine Grace Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda are so simple and comprehensible that by following them the life of a practitioner appears to be one with the goal. I am enclosing a privatization bill. Everything belongs to Kṛṣṇa, and therefore I suppose the Society for Krishna Consciousness knows how to use it. And I would be very glad to help you in some way.

A. F. Tsyvon  
Amur region

Gentlemen,

These days my friends and I are reading one of the books by His Divine Grace Śrīla Swami Prabhupāda: *Life Comes from Life*. We reached the conclusion that we should somehow change our present life. Everything he said in this book shows us what actual human life is, how short it is, and how many things have to be done in this given period of time. We would like to dedicate our lives to real, direct service to God. In all the Vedic literature it is said that no one can attain knowledge of the Lord by speculation and reading books. Therefore we would like to have a spiritual master and renounce the world. We think it is impossible to attain in our city. Could you tell us the addresses of temples? Our intentions are serious.

Oleg Volobuyev  
Belegorsk

## Romapāda Swami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

*mūkaṁ karoti vācālaṁ paṅguṁ laṅghayate girim*  
*yat-kṛpā tam ahaṁ vande śrī-guruṁ dīna-tāraṇam*

In the procession of thousands upon thousands of your followers who are offering praises unto you on this day, your Vyāsa-pūjā, all over the world, I acknowledge my tininess before your oceanic greatness. With sincere, genuine humility, I offer this homage to you, anticipating the cumulative light that will be produced by the many offerings presented to you throughout the day, like so many ghee lamps—thousands and thousands of them—placed ablaze before your *āsana*. I represent but a tiny spark amidst that big blaze of pure devotion.

How can one who is lame cross a mountain? How can one who is dumb speak proper words of praise? How can one who is fallen be delivered? Your mercy is our only hope and shelter.

This is not empty praise!! It is in fact knowable by practical experience. Practically you gave me this understanding in my first days as a new devotee. The process of *bhakti,* of which you are the most merciful deliverer, is extremely pure. Perfectly pure. By your mercy, one can be caught up in the *bhakti* process and become purified by it. Very quickly this can happen. But if one maintains material impurities, those impurities are purged and washed away by the antiseptic property of the high-grade *uttama-bhakti* which you are offering, and such an unfortunate attached soul may also be washed away along with the material impurity unless he remains attached to your unalloyed service.

To my utter amazement I watched this happen, before my new-*bhakta* eyes, shortly after beginning my devotional service under your guidance.

I could understand, even in a young and tender stage, that one had to really want the Real Thing, the actual gift you had to offer—or else the external energy would be at our doorstep, ready to offer another engagement, something less than pure devotion.

Let us consider a moment how this purification works:

*guru-mukha-padma-vākya cittete koriyā-aikya*  
*ār nā koriha mane āśā*

**Narottama dāsa Ṭhākura sings, “Make the teachings emanating from the lotus mouth of the spiritual master one with your heart, and do not desire anything else.”**

Become exclusively established, *koriyā-aikya,* in consciousness, *cittete,* in his *vākya,* or sound vibrations. Fix up your mind in pure devotion NOT to desire anything else.

To the extent that we can accomplish this, we become purified.

How? The instructions of the *guru*—and that is you, Śrīla Prabhupāda—are filled with spiritual potencies. By singularly absorbing our consciousness in your instructions, we become caught up in the rushing current of your purity and spiritual potency, like entering into the current of a swiftly moving river. Without secondary effort, the faithful adherent to your instructions becomes swiftly swept away by the powerful current of pure devotional service found within your utterances.

And for one who acts independently, or in some way apart from your guidance and your personal instructions, what happens? Such actors are either purged of that tendency by your purity—or they become USELESS.

One place where you instruct us about not becoming useless is in connection with Advaita Ācārya’s descendants:

“When disciples do not stick to the principle of accepting the order of their spiritual master, there immediately are two opinions. Any opinion different from the opinion of the spiritual master is useless. … That is deviation.”

A similar warning comes from Lord Caitanya’s lotus mouth:

*loka-lajjā haya, dharma-kīrti haya hāni*  
*aiche karma nā kariha kabhu ihā jāni’*  
*(Cc Ādi 12.52)*

**“Thus one becomes unpopular in the eyes of the people in general, for this damages his religiosity and fame. A Vaiṣṇava, especially one who acts as a spiritual master, must not act in such a way. One should always be conscious of this fact.”**

The chastisement of Kamalākānta Viśvāsa by Lord Caitanya for damaging the religious principles of Advaita Ācārya, and thereby rendering the Ācārya *loka-lajjā,* or unpopular, was understood by “all present … to be meant for everyone.”

Theistic conduct must be our hallmark if we are to stay within your flow of mercy. As Lord Caitanya instructs, “One must always be conscious of this fact.” ALWAYS.

I want to stay always in your all-purifying service, Śrīla Prabhupāda. I don’t want to find myself EVER in a position other than at your feet, worshiping you and executing your instructions.

But I am tiny and feeble, dumb and lame. I want to please you, but I have no qualification to do so. And yet you have given me so much service to do! All of which requires strength, and power of speech, and steadfast adherence to your order, and wisdom beyond my capacity!

I therefore fall at your feet and pray, “Please make me a fit instrument to represent you purely. I pray for this with all my heart and soul. Please guide me to represent your will in word and in action. I want nothing else in this life.”

Please give your whole mercy to one who is so fallen, for without your mercy I am a dead man and useless.

Your eternal servant,  
Romapāda Swami

## Śivarāma Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my humble obeisances at the dust of your divine lotus feet. All glories to Your Divine Grace, the savior of the fallen.

Last fall I visited my mother in Toronto. In the process I witnessed a strong reminder of your kindness. That experience is the basis of this humble offering for the celebration of your 99th appearance day.

After my father’s demise, mother moved from Montreal to share a flat with her sister. On my visit I stayed at our Avenue Road Temple and visited her daily. In good health and spirits, at the fag end of this life, the threshold of another, my mother inhabits a world dominated by memories. Aside from the two children of my cousin, bodily necessities, and trivial entertainment, there are no rewarding events to anchor her to the present. The main thrust of our conversations lay in history. She showed me family albums featuring grandmothers, grandfathers, uncles, aunts—a countless variety of relatives. I shared this common exchange with little interest, solely as a matter of obligation.

“Do you want to see pictures of you?” she asked.

“Hardly interested,” I thought, but replied, “All right.”

She unveiled a stack of albums and opened them one by one. The years of struggle comprising my pre-devotional life passed by in minutes.

There was Peter—  
a naked baby belly-down on a sheet  
at 2 dressed in knitted winter wear (wasn’t he cute?)  
at 5 standing at the doorsteps in shorts, first day at school  
at 10 with friend Tony fishing in the Laurentians  
at 16 high school graduation and on to university  
at 19 the marriage that would last forever  
at 20 working for the summer at a mine in Manitoba  
at 21 in his fourth year at McGill  
at 23 a bearded hippie searching for light  
at 24 the last picture as a “normal” person

These were but some of countless photos, each marking another event in the abject failure of just one of the unlimited conditioned living entities suffering in this world.

“All the other pictures are after you joined to become a Kṛṣṇa,” she said. “Aren’t you sorry you didn’t continue in your profession in the real world?”

I looked up from the photo album. Before me sat the remnants of how many generations of my line? Two elderly ladies struggling with senility, scraps of paper their past, the present governed by disease, the finality of death to come.

“This is all your family,” they said as they pointed to the heaps of photo albums on the table.

I looked at them with incredulity, then back to the collection of discoloring Kodak paper.

Your voice echoed in my heart.

“My spiritual master was no ordinary spiritual master. He saved me.”

\* \* \*

Murāri Caitanya dāsa died of snakebite at an early age, and his parents placed his body in the Ganges. When the great devotee Sāraṅga Ṭhākura took his bath that day, the corpse of the boy brushed against his body. With the strength of his devotion, the Ṭhākura brought Murāri Caitanya back to life. From that time they were inseparable companions.

Hearing of the reappearance of their son, Murāri’s parents came to Sāraṅga Ṭhākura’s *kuṭīra* to claim him.

The boy said. “Why do you claim to be my parents? You had given me up for dead and cast me into the Gaṅgā. Sāraṅga Ṭhākura gave me new life and taught me the service of Lord Caitanya. He is my father, this is my home.”

Under the spell of Māyā, coerced by sweet words into bodily identification, I too was cast by family, lovers, and friends into the irresistible current that is the material world. When I became a “Kṛṣṇa” they had given me up for a dead corpse. But you, like Sāraṅga Ṭhākura, gave me life, service, and love. Now once again death comes with open arms in the form of family enticements. “Now come back home.”

Where is that home? Who is that family?

It is you that saved me from the horrible abyss depicted in the photos before me. You are my only father, friend, and guide. Those who are your servants are my family, and wherever they reside is my home. Strangers are those who after four decades still offer the poison chalice of material enjoyment, albeit with all best intentions.

Yes, you saved me—  
from madness caused by intoxication,  
from senseless toil born of lust,  
from slavery to wife, dog, and home,  
from heartbreaking materialistic values,  
from the loneliness of meaningless efforts,  
from the disappointment of cheating religions.

And I did not even want to be saved! Still you forced the medicine of *hari-nāma* into my unwilling lips.

*vairāgya-yug-bhakti-rasaṁ prayatnair*  
*apāyayan mām anabhīpsum andham*  
*kṛpāmbudhir yaḥ para-duḥkha-duḥkhī*  
*sanātanas taṁ prabhum āśrayāmi*

**“I was unwilling to drink the nectar of devotional service possessed of renunciation, but Sanātana Gosvāmī, out of his causeless mercy, made me drink it, even though I was otherwise unable to do so. Therefore he is an ocean of mercy. He is very compassionate to fallen souls like me, and thus it is my duty to offer my respectful obeisances unto his lotus feet.”**

In my heart rises another picture. It is not to be found in mother’s photo album. It is a mid-spring afternoon. There is Peter sitting in the green Vokswagen, driving toward the temple. Both the reluctant wife and amiable dog have been dropped off at the mother-in-law’s. There are tears in his eyes, his heart pierced by attachment.

Driving, driving through the tears  
Driving away from that world  
Driving as if someone else was in control  
Driving back to Godhead  
Driving or being driven?

On the dashboard the only object in focus, a picture of you. Finally finding himself standing in the center of the *prasādam* room with luggage in hand, he stammers, “I am here, *prabhus*.”

That was half a lifetime ago. Now there are so many more pictures. They are in the photo album deep in my heart. In 1973 I turned on the ignition with a plan to drive to the temple and to Kṛṣṇa. Twenty-two years later I continue to drive, not having yet arrived at my destination. As then, so now, I am reluctant. As then, so now, you are in control, forcing me to keep moving forward. As then, so now, you are saving me.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, you are no ordinary spiritual master. Please continue to save me, despite my stubbornness. Please bring me, the black sheep of your family, struggling desperately to serve you by serving your mission, ever closer to you. It is my only hope to repay your kindness. Please accept my humble effort. Please always keep me among the members of your transcendental family. I have nowhere else to go.

\* \* \*

“Do you want to see any more pictures?”

I looked up at my mother,

“No thank you.”

Then I left.

\* \* \*

Yes, I do want to see more pictures.

I want to always remember, always see the pictures of you, Śrīla Prabhupāda—

- preaching with youthful vigor
- chanting with eyes closed in ecstasy
- sitting on the *vyāsāsana*
- smiling that incomparable smile
- saving the unsavable
- happy that your ISKCON is fulfilling your will
- pleased with all your followers,

even this insignificant person who is trying to become your servant,  
… to whom you will never be an ordinary spiritual master, another spiritual master, or just a spiritual master. You saved me then, and you are saving me now, Śrīla Prabhupāda. You are master of my heart.

Śivarāma Swami

## Śrīdhara Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept this most fallen soul as your servant. I have no qualification to glorify a great soul like you. When I first became your disciple, at least formally, very few people recognized your greatness. Now there are many, and some even admit that you are an empowered (*śaktyāveśa*) *jīva* incarnation of Lord Kṛṣṇa: *kṛṣṇa-śakti vinā nahe tāra pravartana*. I am confident that someday (perhaps sooner than we know) you will be glorified as the most important spiritual personality in this golden period of Kali-yuga besides Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu Himself. Your preaching is expanding everywhere, and it is only natural that as people get relief from their suffering by following your instructions they will want to glorify you.

However, I am embarrassed to say that I find this most auspicious day of your Vyāsa-pūjā awkward. I am never satisfied with any of my attempts to praise you. Your achievements are so numerous. In Bombay on your Vyāsa-pūjā day we place an advertisement in the newspaper with a long list of some of your well-known contributions. I could repeat them here for others to read. I could take one of them and try to focus on it more and more. However, I think I will just offer something from my heart, and I pray you will accept it. It is not exactly direct glorification of you but is my meditation on how I want to serve you.

The most mystical manifestation of your mercy and potency is how you have captured the hearts of so many educated young men and women around the world and engaged them in Lord Caitanya’s mission. I also want to be one of those fortunate souls. I want to be effective in my insignificant attempt to serve you, and therefore I have written down my personal mission statement. I try to review and improve it regularly. Śrīla Prabhupāda, I am feeling great pain in separation from you. You are so kind, merciful, and loving toward your devotees. Please bless me that I may fulfill my mission in life to become your servant.

My mission is to serve my spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, with all my heart and soul. He saved me from drowning in the darkness of ignorance in this miserable material world. He brought the torchlight of knowledge that devotional service to Lord Kṛṣṇa, the Absolute Truth, the Personality of Godhead, is the highest goal of human life. I was forced to open my eyes. To achieve this goal has now become my only reason for living. Out of a great sense of debt for his having shown me the path, I want to become a sharp instrument to be used by Śrīla Prabhupāda in his mission of flooding the world with love of Kṛṣṇa. I will strive to achieve excellence in all my activities, eternal and temporal. I will struggle to rise above my lower animal nature, my conditioning of the lowest of births, and my lack of cultural training. I will strive to become a perfect gentleman, as Śrīla Prabhupāda desired of all his disciples. I will study his books regularly and thoroughly. I will do my best to follow his teachings perfectly and teach others with whom I come in contact according to my own level of realization. I will be forever loyal to his ISKCON, being always positive and ready to help in its development. I will be proactive as a preacher, teacher, facilitator, leader, and spiritual master (servant). I will always be ready to, as Śrīla Prabhupāda put it, “do the needful,” curbing down my false ego, which may act as an obstacle. I will meditate upon and try to imbibe the twenty-six qualities of a pure devotee. I will manage my time to be always active in “Quadrant II” activities of devotional service. I will not over-endeavor but will strive for quality in my work and relationships. I will be constantly on guard against committing offenses to other Vaiṣṇavas. Having realized that in previous years of association with devotees I must have committed so many offenses due to my gross and careless nature, I will sincerely try to recognize those offenses, beg forgiveness from those whom I have offended, and atone for these offenses as best I can. I will continually evaluate my progress, getting input from my peers. Despite all obstacles, personal and incidental, I will not cease from struggling to improve my character and obtain his mercy, to be finally accepted by Śrīla Prabhupāda as his humble servant.

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, I am praying that by writing down and focusing on my mission statement I will be able to glorify you by doing something for you. I know I will have to personally struggle hard to be successful, but statistics have shown this method very effective in the material realm. Perhaps your *Markine Bhāgavata Dharma* was one of your mission statements.

Your servant,  
Śrīdhara Swami

## Suhotra Swami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

**“I offer my respectful obeisances unto His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, who is very dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa, having taken shelter at His lotus feet.”**

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

**“Our respectful obeisances are unto you, O spiritual master, servant of Sarasvatī Gosvāmī. You are kindly preaching the message of Lord Caitanyadeva and delivering the Western countries, which are filled with impersonalism and voidism.”**

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (8.20.21, purport) you write as follows:

“Sometimes we see a devotee offering Ganges water to the Ganges. After taking his bath in the Ganges, a devotee takes a palmful of water and offers it back to the Ganges. Actually, when one takes a palmful of water from the Ganges, the Ganges does not lose anything, and similarly if a devotee offers a palmful of water to the Ganges, the Ganges does not increase in any way. But by such an offering, the devotee becomes celebrated as a devotee of mother Ganges. Similarly, when we offer anything with devotion and faith, what we offer does not belong to us, nor does it enrich the opulence of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. But if one offers whatever he has in his possession, he becomes a recognized devotee.”

All I have in my possession is the sublime knowledge you have given me. In the same way that a palmful of Ganges water is offered to the Ganges, I shall attempt to offer a palmful of knowledge into the ocean of philosophical truth that is Your Divine Grace with this homage. It is based upon a room conversation you had with a German man and a professor in Mexico City, January, 1975, edited here for brevity’s sake. I’ve made notes on certain points according to my palmful of realization, which exists only by your mercy .

The topic under discussion is “Why is there anything?”

**Guest (1) (German man):** I would like to ask you a question. Once Leibnitz, who is one of the fathers of the Western tradition, formulated the question which was the beginning of metaphysics in a way, Western metaphysics. The question is “Why there is anything?” What is your stand about this classic point?

Note: According to G. W. Leibnitz (1646–1716), the “why” of anything cannot be discerned by studying anything, because the things of this world do not contain in themselves the reason for their existence. The reason for anything is to be found in the being that does contain the reason for its own existence, i.e., the “Necessary Being,” God. The complete reason for anything is knowable to the mind of God, not to the mind of man. Therefore, God’s mind is perfect. But Leibnitz’s argument for God’s perfection does not proceed further, from the cosmological to the transcendental. He posits God’s mind as nothing more than a mirror of this imperfect cosmos. He pleads that the perfection of the cosmos is that it is mirrored in the perfect mind of God. This perfection is to be approached through abstract mathematics. Leibnitz’s attempt to answer the question “why” leads to further “why’s.” We shall see why it is so in a moment.

**Prabhupāda:** \[chuckles\] “Why anything exists?” \[laughter\] What do you mean by “anything”?

**Guest (1):** Well, that’s precisely the point. What is the purpose? What is the sense, if there is any, or does the very question make sense?

**Prabhupāda:** No, no, unless understand what is that “anything” … First of all, you have to understand what is that “anything.” Anything … just like this book, this table, this bell, the electric fan, they are so many things. So you can take any one of them; that is anything. What is your idea of anything?

Note: His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, in these few matter-of-fact sentences, calls attention to a grave shortcoming in Leibnitz’s approach to philosophy. A passage from a textbook explains: “Leibnitz’s logical doctrine … remains a logical doctrine with no bearing on the nature of what there is in the world.” Leibnitz believed that the mind is not functionally connected to the world of matter. Mind and matter are two chains of events initiated by God, working independently of each other. His “reason for anything” is simply the mathematical harmony of these two chains of events. Such a “reason why” does not pertain to any thing described by words like a book, table, or bell. Therefore it cannot answer questions about the purpose of the objects of our perception. Sense objects are precisely the “anything” that we know.

**Guest (1):** Oh, reality. Material, external, reality to our ego, our internal reality as well.

**Prabhupāda:** Internal reality and external reality?

**Guest (1):** Both. For me, the word “anything” covers both.

**Prabhupāda:** Yes. So that also we understand, “anything.” There are so many varieties of things, and you can take any one of them. That is “anything.” But your question should be, “Wherefrom these things are coming?” That should be the proper question.

**Professor:** What is the reason of this \[indistinct\] “anything”?

**Prabhupāda:** Yes. There are so many things, and you can take any one of them. That is “anything.” But the real question should be, “Wherefrom all these things are coming?” That is real question, “What is the origin of all these things?”

Note: In the *Bhāgavatam* (11.22.34), the attempt to establish by argument the reality and unreality of the things of this world is condemned by Lord Kṛṣṇa as useless, although thinkers who are not Kṛṣṇa conscious are unable to give it up. Leibnitz argued that the reality of “anything” is an abstract state of calculation in harmony with calculations about any and all other things. Apart from this, “nothing” is real. But this is only one of many ways to think about “anything” and “nothing.” Perhaps “nothing” really exists out there, and “anything” that we take to be real is just a creation of deluded perception and speculation. Or perhaps “anything” is real, with “nothing” dependent upon our power of perception and thought. These arguments are as endless as they are useless. Therefore Śrīla Prabhupāda turns the guest’s attention to a different concern, which Western thinkers frame with the word “teleology.” The teleological argument for the existence of God asserts that an intelligent comprehension of the things of the cosmos requires the acknowledgment of an intelligent origin of everything.

**Guest (1):** Well, origin, that is more on the theoretical side. It’s a question, “Why?” But I am, rather, after the purpose.

Note: The guest has not yet grasped the significance of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s reply. The word “teleology” is built upon the Greek root *telos,* which means “purpose,” “goal,” or “end.” Teleology is precisely knowledge of the purpose of anything, which the guest claims he is after. Our Kṛṣṇa conscious teleological stand is that the purpose of anything is known in the intent of its creator.

**Prabhupāda:** Yes. That is a nice question. But there is the real source of everything. That is in the *Vedānta-sūtra* … Perhaps you have read. *Vedānta-sūtra,* first question is: “Wherefrom all these things come?” So the answer is that *janmādy asya yataḥ:* “Brahman. The original thing is Brahman, or the Absolute Truth, and from Him everything is emanating.”

Note: Śrīla Prabhupāda’s phrase, “there is the real source of everything,” is most significant. The real source can only be Brahman, the unchanging *sat*. *Nāsato vidyate bhāvaḥ:* that which does not endure (the material world) is *asat,* or unreal, and therefore cannot be the real cause of anything. Real philosophy must identify that origin (*ādi*) which is eternal and unchanging. “So science, philosophy, means to find out the ultimate cause of everything. That we are getting from the *śāstras,* Vedic literature, that Kṛṣṇa is the cause of all causes” (Śrīla Prabhupāda lecture, 21 August 1973, in London).

**Prabhupāda:** Just like physical … The sun is there, and whole material world is product of the sunshine. What your physical science says? Eh? Eh? Do they not say? It is a fact that sunshine … due to the sunshine all these material things are there.

**Guest (1):** Well, it’s more involved than just saying that … about the meaning of celestial bodies and the meaning of, in particular, of sun and moon and so on … We can’t see, assuming all the glory of that what happens on the earth due to the existence of those bodies, we do not try to look inside of the structure of these things, as something meant for us. Just universe as it is … And this question, like the Nietzchean question which I am repeating—that’s not my point—this big question is … Rarely, directly, we hear the direct answer to that.

Note: The guest doesn’t complete his thought about the “Nietzchean question.” It seems he is referring to Nietzche’s calling into question the idea that the things of the world have an innate human-centered purpose or truth to them. In *The Will to Power,* Nietzche argued that the world is “as a falsehood always changing but never getting near the truth: for there is no ‘truth.’” For Nietzche, the world is untrue because the world separates humankind from reason, the way a veil separates the face of a woman from the eyes of a man. Yet he wondered (in *The Joyful Wisdom*) if there is truth in this very veiling of the truth: “Perhaps truth is a woman who has reasons for not showing her reasons?” Not knowing that this feminine *tattva* is Kṛṣṇa’s *śakti,* Nietzche could not know her telos, or true purpose: devotional service to Kṛṣṇa. She serves Kṛṣṇa by veiling the truth of the Lord and His energies from the eyes of the nondevotees, and lifting the veil of Māyā from the eyes of the devotees.

**Professor:** If Indian philosophy …

**Prabhupāda:** No, no, it is no Indian or American. It is the philosophy. It is philosophy. The philosophy is not Indian or American. Truth is truth, not Indian truth or American truth. That is not truth. That is relative truth. The Absolute Truth is absolute. That is neither Indian nor American.

Note: As noted before, real philosophy concerns itself with the intelligent origin (*ādi*) of everything, not with speculative arguments over what is real and not real about anything. All Western philosophy began with Greek philosophy. Greek philosophy began as a dispute between those who argued that the *arche,* or origin, is chaos and those who argued it is *logos*. Today, Western materialists still posit chaos as the origin of everything. They shift the sense of the word “chaos” away from the original Greek, so as to mean a state of disorder and unreason, or a void. But the original sense of “chaos” is better reflected in the English word “chasm”: a gap. Chaos is the “gap between,” just as *pradhāna* is the unmanifest between the spiritual and the material manifestations in Vedānta philosophy. *Pradhāna* is unconscious and is represented in the state of dreamless sleep, called *suṣupti*. Similarly, in Hesiod’s Theogony, night, sleep, and death are said to represent chaos. *Logos* has two senses: meaningful sound (this sense of *logos* is evident in the word “dialogue”), and reasonable thought (as evident in the word “logic”). In Vedānta philosophy, creation is the order brought to *pradhāna* by the thoughts of Mahā-Viṣṇu, transmitted by *śabda,* Vedic sound. “This material world is created by the dreaming of Mahā-Viṣṇu” (*Bhāg.* 4.29.83, purport); “The material energy is coming through the breathing of Mahā-Viṣṇu, who is lying down in the Kāraṇa, Causal Ocean. So, from the breathing there is sound” (Śrīla Prabhupāda *Bhagavad-gītā* lecture, 19 February 1974, Bombay). The idea that intelligent *logos* is the *arche,* or origin, is an ancient Greek attempt at “real philosophy,” Vedānta. The opposing idea that everything originates from unintelligent chaos resembles atheistic Sāṅkhya philosophy.

**Guest (1):** But in what sense do you use the concept “truth” here? Is it in the ontological sense, or is it in somehow in a more pragmatical human sense, refers to human beings or … ?

**Prabhupāda:** Yes, it is pragmatic, that you cannot see beyond this wall. That is your insufficient knowledge, or your senses are insufficient. You cannot go beyond this wall. But that does not mean there is nothing beyond this wall. So if you want to know what is beyond this wall, you have to know from a person who knows it. Yes. Because you cannot see, you cannot know, that is not the end. There must be something.

Note: Pragmatism is a doctrine that favors “value” over abstract “truth.” The value of anything is the effect it has on the conduct of life. Pragmatic evaluation of anything supports regular conduct of life, as expressed in the Greek verb *prassein,* “to do (habitually),” from which the word pragmatic is derived. Nietzche summed up his view of pragmatism in these statements from *Thus Spake Zarathustra:* “Many lands saw Zarathustra, and many peoples: thus he discovered the good and the bad of many peoples. … Much that passed for good with one people was regarded with scorn and contempt by another. … Much here called bad was there decked with purple honors.” In other words, the values of pragmatism, being human-centered, are inherently relative. In Vedānta philosophy, regular conduct of life means the religious rites prescribed in the *Vedas*. The *Vedas* are *apauruṣeya,* not conceived by human beings. Therefore the sacrificial duties given therein are called *setu,* “the bridge” between the relative world and the transcendental world. As Lord Kṛṣṇa explains in the *Bhagavad-gītā* (4.33), *sarvaṁ karmākhilaṁ pārtha jñāne parisamāpyate:* “All sacrifices of work culminate in transcendental knowledge.” Now a new doubt arises. Transcendental knowledge awaits us on the other side of the bridge of Vedic *dharma*. Still, while on this side, we must know and embrace the values that support that bridge. Otherwise how can we, who are blinded by the ignorance of our relative human-centered values, value the crossing of that bridge? As Śrīla Prabhupāda said to the guest, “It is pragmatic, that you cannot see beyond this wall. That is your insufficient knowledge, or your senses are insufficient.” Prabhupāda gives a transcendentally pragmatic solution to the problem of humanistic pragmatism: “If you want to know what is beyond this wall, you have to know from a person who knows it.” Kṛṣṇa similarly declares in the *Bhagavad-gītā* (4.34) that knowledge of the transcendental goal of prescribed duties is given by a *tattva-darśī*. Because the *tattva-darśī* sees the truth, he can lead the conditioned soul across the bridge of the *Vedas*.

**Professor:**  But in the case of a mystical man that has been able to see …

**Prabhupāda:**  There is no question of mystic. First of all we have to admit that on account of our senses being imperfect, whatever knowledge we gather, that is imperfect. That is imperfect. Therefore, if you want to possess real knowledge you have to approach somebody who is perfect.

Note: Śrīla Prabhupāda uses the word “mysticism” in different contexts. The context here, in which mysticism is rejected, is summed up in the following sentence from the *Bhāgavatam* (1.12.3, purport): “Ardent hearing from the bona fide master is the only way to receive transcendental knowledge, and there is no need for medical performances or occult mysticism for miraculous effects.” The context in which mysticism is acceptable is seen next. “Vedānta is the last word in Vedic wisdom, and the author and knower of the Vedānta philosophy is Lord Kṛṣṇa; and the highest Vedāntist is the great soul who takes pleasure in chanting the holy name of the Lord. That is the ultimate purpose of all Vedic mysticism” (*Bhagavad-gītā* 2.46, purport). The Vedic context of mysticism (*rāja-guhyam,* the secret of all secrets) is explained by Lord Kṛṣṇa in the *Bhagavad-gītā* (Chapter 9). In text 9.1 He prefaces this explanation by reminding Arjuna, *pravakṣyāmi,* “I am speaking.” What Kṛṣṇa speaks in the *Bhagavad-gītā* constitutes real mysticism, for He is Yogeśvara, the master of all mystics.

**Guest (1):**  How can we know that somebody is perfect?

**Prabhupāda:** That is another thing. But first of all, the basic principle is we have to understand that our senses are imperfect, and whatever knowledge we gather by these imperfect senses, they are imperfect. So if we want perfect knowledge, then we have to approach somebody whose senses are perfect, whose knowledge is perfect. That is the principle. That is the Vedic principle.

Therefore the Vedic principle says, *tad-vijñānārthaṁ sa gurum evābhigacchet*. You know Sanskrit, yes. “In order to know that perfect knowledge, one should approach *guru*.” So who is *guru*? Then the next question will be … Your question is that, “How I can?”

**Professor:** Well, this is … we’ve only come to one of the mentioned theories of knowledge, I think, *śabda*.

**Prabhupāda:** *Śabda,* yes, *śabda-brahma*. Yes … just like many thousands of miles away we are getting some radio message and we learn that “Something is happening there. Something is there.” Therefore *śabda*. This is … *śabda* means sound, sound, sound vibration. So that is the real source of knowledge. That is the real source of … *śabda-brahma*.

**Professor:** One of the sources of knowledge or the only one?

**Prabhupāda:** No, that is the only one. There are others; they are subordinate. But the *śabda,* knowledge received, *śabda,* through *śabda, śabda-brahma,* that is perfect knowledge. Just like the same example: beyond this wall I cannot see, but if somebody there says, “This is the position here”—the sound comes—that is perfect. You cannot see what is going on, but if somebody says, sends radio message or any message, sound, then you know. Therefore *śabda-pramāṇa, śabda,* knowledge received through *śabda,* that is perfect knowledge. … Direct perception. *Śabda,* you can \[have\] direct perception. It is not intuition. It is perception. … So this is *śabda-pramāṇa, śabda-pramāṇa,* that “In the beginning there was God, nothing else.” So in this way our Vedic principle is: when your knowledge is corroborated by the Vedic version, then it is perfect.

Note: The word *pramāṇa* in Sanskrit means “source of valid knowledge.” Three kinds of *pramāṇa* are accepted in the Brahma-Madhva-Gauḍīya-sampradāya. These are *śabda* (spiritual sound), *pratyakṣa* (sensory perception), and *anumāna* (logical argument). The purpose of *pramāṇa* is *pramā* (correct cognition); therefore *pramāṇa* is that by which the truth is ascertained. But the *ācāryas* of our *sampradāya* hold that of the three *pramāṇas, śabda* alone is the best evidence because it alone reveals the truth that lies beyond material perception. Therefore Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “That \[*śabda*\] is the only one. There are others; they are subordinate.” Sensory perception (*pratyakṣa*) and logic (*anumāna*) help us only to understand the Vedic evidence in terms of our present condition. But they themselves are unable to reveal eternal truths. As Prabhupāda said, “*Śabda,* you can \[have\] direct perception \[that\] in the beginning there was God, nothing else.” Then in what way are sensory perception and logic accepted as evidence? In that with their help, Vedic knowledge can be demonstrated and confirmed. When employed skillfully, sensory perception and logic reinforce faith in the *śabda-pramāṇa* (evidence of spiritual sound). *Pratyakṣa* and *anumāna* that do not support the *śabda* as enunciated by the *tattva-darśī* are to be rejected.

**Professor:** But why existence of all these things?
